--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
## Reasoning

The champion (-13.38) is a 2-state machine:
- State 0: Exploration (rwm=19, slow turning), LED cyan, 3 transitions all going to state 1 (D=0 with 2 states means the other state)
- State 1: GoAwayColor(green=2), LED magenta, 3 transitions all going back to state 0

The key insight: GoAwayColor(green) moves away from the green wall, which means moving toward the nest (red wall side). When on black floor (source area), robot gets an item, then goes away from green = toward nest. This effectively delivers blue items (the good ones) more than green ones because the robot approaches from the non-green side.

Second best (-12.52) is same with rwm=15. Third best (-2.95) uses explicit GoToBlue→GoToRed foraging.

**Exploitation strategy:**
1. Champion verbatim (elitism)
2. rwm variations: 10, 12, 17, 22, 25
3. Probability tweaks on champion transitions
4. Add white floor trigger to state 0 (to leave nest area faster)
5. Add black floor trigger to state 1 (to keep going away from green after pickup)

**Exploration strategy:**
- 3-state: explore→GoAwayGreen→GoToBlue (with floor-based switching)
- Champion-like but with GoAwayGreen in state 0 and explore in state 1
- Add a third state for explicit nest-seeking

With 2 states, D=0 is always the other state.

## Reasoning

The champion (-14.00) is the same 2-state structure but with p0x0=0.95 and p0x1=0.5 (higher probabilities of transitioning to state 1). The core structure is:
- State 0: Exploration (rwm=19), 3 transitions → state 1 (via prob 0.95, prob 0.5, neighbors>8)
- State 1: GoAwayColor(green=2), 3 transitions → state 0 (via prob 0.9692, black floor 0.7499, perceive-magenta 0.8499)

Key observations:
- All top machines share this same 2-state topology with GoAwayGreen
- The champion improved by raising p0x0 from 0.8989→0.95 and p0x1 from 0.4698→0.5
- The 4-transition state 0 variant (rank 2) also performed well
- rwm=19 seems optimal (rwm=15 and rwm=25 both slightly worse)

**Exploitation:** Vary probabilities around champion values, try different neighbor thresholds, try p0x0 even higher (0.97, 0.99), vary p0x1 (0.4, 0.55, 0.6), vary p1x1 (black floor probability).

**Exploration:** Try adding white-floor transition to state 0, try 3-state with the champion core plus a GoToRed state, try different w0x2 values.

## Reasoning

The champion (-14.00) remains unchanged. All round 4 machines scored between -12.17 and -13.52, none beating -14.00. The topology is firmly established: 2-state, Explore(rwm=19) + GoAwayGreen(clr=2).

Key observations from round 4:
- p0x0=0.99, p0x1=0.6 scored -13.52 (rank 1 this round but not champion)
- p0x0=0.97, p0x1=0.5 scored -13.45 (rank 2)
- p0x0=0.95, p0x1=0.5 is the champion
- Higher p0x0 seems good (0.95-0.99 range)
- p0x1=0.5 seems optimal (0.4 and 0.55 both worse)
- p0x2=8 (neighbor threshold) seems best
- w0x2=11.1097 seems good

The champion parameters seem very well-tuned. I need to:
1. Keep champion verbatim
2. Exploit: fine-tune around champion values - try p0x0=0.96, 0.93, p0x1=0.48, 0.52, different w0x2
3. Explore: try genuinely different structures to escape if this is a local optimum - 3-state machines, different color targets, etc.

For exploration, the 3-state machine (rank 8, -12.93) shows promise. Let me try variants with better parameterization.

Also note: the champion has the neighbor-above condition (C=3) in state 0. Maybe trying C=4 (below threshold) would give different dynamics.

Let me try some exploration with GoAwayColor(blue=3) instead of green, and also try GoToColor(red=1) explicitly.

## Reasoning

The champion (-14.00) remains at p0x0=0.95, p0x1=0.5, rwm=19, w0x2=11.1097, p0x2=8 (neighbor threshold). Five rounds of fine-tuning haven't beaten it. The scores cluster tightly between -12.5 and -13.5 for all variants, suggesting we're near a local optimum.

Key observations:
- p0x0=0.95 seems optimal (0.93 worse, 0.96/0.97/0.99 all slightly worse)
- p0x1=0.5 seems optimal (0.48 and 0.52 both slightly worse)
- rwm=19 seems best (18, 20, 25 all worse)
- w0x2=11.1097 seems best (9, 13 both similar but slightly worse)
- p1x1 (black floor prob)=0.7499 seems good; 0.70 and 0.85 worse
- The 3-state extension doesn't help

I need to:
1. Keep champion verbatim
2. Try some genuinely different explorations - we're stuck
3. Try modifying state 1's transition structure more radically
4. Try different color in state 1 (GoAwayBlue instead of GoAwayGreen)
5. Try swapping which color is avoided
6. Try adding a gray floor transition
7. Try completely different 3-state or 4-state architectures

The key insight I haven't fully explored: maybe the robot should go TOWARD the blue wall (GoToColor blue=3) rather than AWAY from green. Or perhaps try GoToColor(red=1) as the return behavior in a 3-state machine. Let me try more radical explorations while keeping fine-tuning variants.

Also: p1x2 (perceive-magenta probability) = 0.8499 - this fires when robot sees magenta (color 5). Since state 1 LED is magenta, robots in state 1 can see each other! This creates a social signal. Let me try varying this.

## Reasoning

1. **Replacing p0x1=prob(0.5) with perceive-magenta(0.8, l=5)** scored -13.92 (very close to champion!) - this is the best variant found. The robot switches to GoAwayGreen when it sees other robots in state 1 (magenta).
2. The 3-state extension of this also scored -13.92.
3. p0x1=perceive-magenta with p=0.9 scored -13.06 (worse), p=0.7 scored -12.59 (worse). So p=0.8 is sweet spot.
4. p1x0=0.9692 seems optimal (0.95 and 0.98 both worse).

New strategy: combine both good features - keep p0x0=0.95 (prob) AND use perceive-magenta for p0x1. Try different probabilities for the perceive-magenta transition. Also try varying p0x2 neighbor threshold with this new structure.

Also: the perceive-magenta transition in state 0 (l0x1=5) creates social coordination - when robots see other GoAwayGreen robots (magenta), they join them. This is effective.

5. Try the perceive-magenta variant with different w0x2 values

## Reasoning

The champion (-14.00) still holds after 8 rounds. The perceive-magenta variant (p0x1=7, l=5) scored -13.92 in round 7 but only -12.63 in round 8 (high variance!). The champion's original p0x1=prob(0.5) is more reliable.

Key observations:
- Round 8 best was only -12.95, much worse than champion - high variance in scores
- The perceive-magenta variant (p=0.8, 0.82, 0.85) scores are noisy (-12.95 to -13.92 across rounds)
- Champion parameters (p0x0=0.95, p0x1=0.5 prob, rwm=19, w0x2=11.1097, p0x2=8) remain best
- We need genuine exploration to escape this local optimum

With only 2 rounds left, I should:
1. Keep champion verbatim (elitism)
2. Run the champion again (to confirm score)
3. Try the best perceive-magenta variants again (they had good rounds)
4. Try some genuinely different topologies that haven't been tried
5. Try varying rwm more (e.g., 30, 50) with champion params
6. Try GoToColor(red=1) as first state instead of exploration

The score -14.00 was achieved by the champion. The theoretical max items is 20 robots × some trips per run. Let me try to push further with genuinely different structures.

New ideas:
- Try state 0 with GoToColor(blue=3) to actively seek source, then GoAwayGreen to return
- Try 3-state with explicit source-seeking
- Try varying rwm drastically (5, 50)
- Try different w0x2 steepness values

################################################################################
# PROMPT SYSTEME
################################################################################

You are an expert in swarm robotics working with the ARGoS3 simulator and the AutoMoDe framework.
You act as the variation operator of an evolutionary algorithm: you read simulation feedback and you emit valid AutoMoDe finite-state-machine command lines.

# 1. THE MISSION
The score is MINIMISED.

The arena contains a nest area at one end, with a WHITE floor, and a source area at the opposite end, with a BLACK floor. The floor in between is GRAY. The wall on the nest side is RED.

The source area is divided into two halves. The wall blocks behind one half are BLUE and those behind the other half are GREEN.

A robot carries at most one item. Entering a half of the source gives the robot an item of that half's type, replacing any item it was already carrying. Entering the nest with an item delivers it: an item from one half decreases the score by 1, an item from the other half increases it by 1.

# 2. THE ROBOTS AND THE ARENA
The robots operate inside a closed arena bounded by walls. 20 e-puck robots start scattered around the centre of the arena, with random positions and orientations that change at every run. Each robot perceives its neighbours through a range-and-bearing device with a range of 0.50 m: neighbour-count conditions only count robots closer than that. A run lasts 1200 simulation steps (120 s at 10 steps per second).

# 3. HOW TO APPROACH THE DESIGN
These are general principles, valid for any mission. They are not a solution to this one.
- Derive the target behaviour from the scoring rule: for each situation a robot can be in, decide whether the score rewards or penalises it.
- Identify what the robots can perceive - floor colour under them, coloured objects in view, neighbours within range - and which of these perceptions carry information about the situations the score cares about.
- Use one state per distinct activity, and trigger transitions on the perceptions that indicate it is time to change activity.

# 4. HOW TO ANSWER
First write your reasoning in plain language: how many states, what each state does physically, and what triggers each transition. Two or three sentences per machine is enough.
Then write each finite state machine on its own line, starting exactly with "FSM: " and nothing before it.

# 5. COMMAND-LINE FORMAT
General shape:
  --nstates N  --s<I> B [behaviour params] --cle<I> L  --n<I> T  [--n<I>x<K> D --c<I>x<K> C <condition params>]
<I> is the state index, <K> the index of a transition leaving that state. Both start at 0.

## A. Global
* `--nstates N` : number of states, integer in [1..4].

## B. Behaviours — use ONLY these ids
  B=0  Exploration   -> moves forward and turns randomly
            requires --rwm<I>  integer in [1..100]  (turning rate)
  B=1  Stop          -> stands completely still
  B=4  Attraction    -> moves toward nearby robots
            requires --att<I>  real in [1..5]  (attraction gain)
  B=5  Repulsion     -> moves away from nearby robots
            requires --rep<I>  real in [1..5]  (repulsion gain)
  B=8  GoToColor     -> moves toward the nearest object of a target colour
            requires --vel<I>  fixed value 1.0  (speed)
            requires --clr<I>  one of {1,2,3,4,5,6}  (target colour)
  B=9  GoAwayColor   -> moves away from the nearest object of a target colour
            requires --vel<I>  fixed value 1.0  (speed)
            requires --clr<I>  one of {1,2,3,4,5,6}  (target colour)

Every state also takes an LED colour:
* `--cle<I> L` : one of {0,4,5,6}  (0=off, 1=red, 2=green, 3=blue, 4=yellow, 5=magenta, 6=cyan)

## C. Transitions
* `--n<I> T` : number of transitions leaving state <I>, integer in [1..4].
  Up to 4 transitions may leave the same state, each with a different condition type. A state that reacts to several different situations is often stronger than a state with one transition, and random sampling produces that variety by default.
* `--n<I>x<K> D` : destination, indexed among the OTHER states only.
  With 3 states, leaving state 1 the others are [0, 2]: D=0 targets state 0, D=1 targets state 2.
  With 2 states, leaving state 0 the only other state is state 1, so D must be 0.
* `--c<I>x<K> C` : condition type — use ONLY these ids
  C=0  floor under the robot is BLACK
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=1  floor under the robot is GRAY
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=2  floor under the robot is WHITE
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=3  number of neighbours ABOVE a threshold
            requires --p<I>x<K>  integer in [1..10]  (threshold: an INTEGER count of neighbours, not a probability)
            requires --w<I>x<K>  real in [0..20]  (steepness of the sigmoid)
  C=4  number of neighbours BELOW a threshold
            requires --p<I>x<K>  integer in [1..10]  (threshold: an INTEGER count of neighbours, not a probability)
            requires --w<I>x<K>  real in [0..20]  (steepness of the sigmoid)
  C=5  fires with a fixed probability at every step
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=7  the robot PERCEIVES a given colour with its camera
            requires --l<I>x<K>  one of {1,2,3,4,5,6}  (colour to look for)
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])

# 6. HARD RULES
1. Integers stay integers; real-valued parameters always carry a decimal point (1.0, not 1).
2. Every state that exists must declare `--n<I>`, and every declared transition must carry its destination, its condition type and that condition's parameters. A missing parameter crashes the simulator.
3. `--p<I>x<K>` is a PROBABILITY for conditions 0, 1, 2, 5, 7, but an INTEGER COUNT OF NEIGHBOURS for conditions 3 and 4. Never write 0.8 where a neighbour count is expected.
4. Never point a transition at its own state.

# 7. YOU ARE THE EVOLUTIONARY OPERATOR
This is a multi-round conversation on one real ARGoS3 optimisation run. The numerical optimiser is restarted from scratch at the beginning of every round, so exploration and exploitation are entirely up to you.

1. SURVIVAL (elitism): copy the current best-ever machine verbatim in your answer so its lineage is not lost.
2. EXPLOITATION: spend about half of your machines on small edits of the best performers — shift a probability, a threshold or a gain by roughly ten percent, and keep the topology.
3. EXPLORATION: spend the rest on genuinely different topologies or module combinations, to escape local optima.
4. RICHNESS: do not let every state settle on a single transition of the same condition type. Include candidates with two to four transitions per state mixing different condition types.
